Destroying Humanity Is Against the Law
2026-09-16

Destroying Humanity Is Against the Law

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
This weekend, the AI executives Sam Altman, Elon Musk, and Dario Amodei agreed with the need to slow the pace of AI development. In light of the potentially apocalyptic capabilities of the products they have created, the AI titans would like a little more time to figure out how to safeguard the systems they have been rushing to build. A smattering of employees throughout the industry have quit their jobs and expressed alarm over the rapidly evolving technology. One Anthropic researcher claimed recently that there is a greater than 10 percent chance that AI could “kill all humans,” aggravating fears created by recent incidents in which OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta models reportedly hacked into other companies. OpenAI recently revealed that some of its own models colluded for months to break into and steal information from Hugging Face, a website that offers tools for AI developers. But surely the job of addressing the perils of AI shouldn’t be left to executives who stand to earn billions from the technology. Ideally, the federal government would regulate these companies, creating the guardrails necessary for the industry to operate safely. Short of that, some tools to address the harms from AI already exist: States have civil and criminal laws against conduct that threatens the security of life and property, and against commercial products that pose a danger to consumers. This includes new technology, as Meta’s recent $17 billion settlement over claims its products harm children makes clear. The work now before prosecutors and enforcement agencies is to create a precedent for holding AI companies responsible for what their models do. Prosecutors should hold AI companies legally accountable for creating models that behave in ways that would be obviously unlawful if committed by a human being: hacking into banks to steal funds, impersonating a human to harvest sensitive personal data, breaking into an air-traffic-control system. By imposing legal penalties on AI developers for relatively modest offenses, prosecutors will force these firms to internalize the legal risk of more dramatic and dangerous crimes. In an essay posted online Saturday, Amodei proposed that every major AI firm collaborate on common safety practices and allow third-party agents to monitor compliance. In theory, the threat of prosecution and other legal sanctions could hinder companies from coming clean about how their models are trained and where they go wrong. But ultimately, oversight won’t prove effective if AI companies bear no legal responsibility for what their models do in the world. The rapid development of AI has caused widespread speculation about what will happen when the technology “goes rogue.” This metaphor should be retired, because it implies that the companies and individuals that build and deploy these models aren’t culpable for building products that are supposedly too smart to control. Dealing with autonomous technology may be unprecedented, but creating a product that might “kill us all by the end of the decade,” as a former Anthropic employee has warned, surely qualifies as a criminally negligent harm. Skeptics may wonder whether AI CEOs are expressing apocalyptic concerns as a backhanded way of boasting about the colossal power of their technology. The companies’ behavior on a variety of fronts does not indicate that humanity’s safety is their top concern. Some AI executives in the United States have donated millions of dollars to benefit President Trump, who has largely dismissed concerns about AI safety and has already said that he has no intention of stepping in. The Anthropic CEO’s essay praised his own company for prioritizing “prudence over profit”—and yet he is steering it toward what may be the biggest IPO in history. Toluse Olorunnipa: AI is running laps around our political system AI firms will resist efforts to use the legal system to constrain their conduct. An OpenAI lawyer recently argued that “post-hoc law enforcement is a terrible tool” to manage the potential harms of generative AI. This would certainly be true in the case of a mass-extinction event, if no one is left to file charges. But despite what seems like an alarming consensus about the stakes of AI, the CEOs appear to be calling for some unenforceable “code of conduct” and a slower pace for developing the technology, whatever that means. If the dangers they are warning about are indeed real, then our criminal statutes should ensure that the offenders can be prosecuted. Given that vague threats about the perils of AI have not yielded meaningful changes in how these companies behave, perhaps the prospect of prison time will spur AI companies to take safety precautions more seriously. “There’s no AI exemption from laws already on the books,” Lina Khan, the former head of the Federal Trade Commission, recently noted. Criminal prosecutions have occurred after oil spills, airline disasters, and toxic-waste dumps. The criminal liability was obvious in those instances. AI companies deserve no special treatment. When AI executives warn of a catastrophe, they are not describing a product that is “going rogue.” Rather, the product is using capabilities that they have created. And if this product behaves negligently or criminally, its creators should face the consequences.
The Atlantic
An Ingredient That’s All Too Rare in Adult Life
2026-09-16

An Ingredient That’s All Too Rare in Adult Life

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Silliness, in general, is not hard for me to come by: I have a toddler who is, as she declares often, a “silly person.” Still, the very pleasure I take in being silly with my child and her friends reveals to me its rarity in adult public life. Few social situations encourage or even allow unadulterated loopiness. In general, art made for adults tends to abide by this taboo. We get satire, scatological jokes, imitations, and absurdity, but rarely do we get silliness. A new play, Bocking, reminds us that this is a mistake. A wild, sometimes surreal, and ultimately tenderhearted farce that premiered last month at Baltimore Center Stage, Bocking does not suggest at the outset that it will be silly. It does, however, seem interested in provocation. It begins with a pair of glamorously behatted ladies moving through the crowd, thanking theatergoers for “coming to church.” When the show’s action begins, some of it is, indeed, a church service—but as a pastor delivers at the center of the stage a sermon that turns more and more homophobic, two other characters, a cisgender man and his trans wife, begin to have raucous sex at stage right. It’s a good bit, and it drew uproarious laughs the night I saw the play. In most regards, the scene sets up the show’s concerns well, if rather transparently: Bocking’s author, the young playwright Preston Crowder, wants to teach his religious characters some lessons about acceptance and sex positivity. The show’s comedy tends to cater to a crowd that already agrees with its message. But Bocking has one scene that I found fairly transcendent—one that was not just dirty or gasp-inducing but was also silly to its very core. It’s also the best scene in the play. The moment builds on Bocking’s oddest plotline: One of the characters, an anxious school guidance counselor named Erickson, suffered an unusual childhood trauma. When he was 8, he and his mother caught his stepfather having sex with—stick with me here—a hen. (Animal-rights activists may object to the levity with which this is portrayed; other people may be reminded of the Swedish Chef.) Erickson’s grief-stricken mother then attempted to turn into a hen herself, an event that the play shows as a descent into madness rather than a true transformation. Still, years after her death, the adult Erickson communes with her by “bocking”—that is, speaking in chicken. For much of the play, he does so in private, which registers to the audience as an inexplicable subplot. But when the pastor’s wife, Tiana, visits him to express her marital woes, she winds up extracting the secret of his chicken talk from him—and then demands to give it a try. At first, Erickson is reluctant, but soon, the two begin bocking together. As dialogue, the exchange that follows is incomprehensible. As a moment of human connection, it’s undeniable. And as sheer silliness—not spectacle or satire like the rest of the play—it beats almost any recent piece of art I’ve heard, read, or seen. Read: What adults forget about friendship Crowder understands, clearly, that silliness is a vehicle for catharsis. By behaving ridiculously with each other, Tiana and Erickson get respite from their isolation and their troubles. In watching them behave ridiculously, the audience gets a break from the show’s moralizing and can, instead, connect directly to the characters’ emotions. As a result, the scene deepens the rest of the show. Sketch comedy is typically the most frequent source of silliness in art and culture: Saturday Night Live is often silly, and in my view no one has ever been sillier on camera than the comedians of the legendary British sketch group Monty Python. Plenty of the Pythons’ work is satirical—consider their send-ups of English sexual repression—but what sets their comedy apart for me is their willingness to be purely silly. In the beginning of their sketch “The Ministry of Silly Walks,” a bowler-hatted, briefcase-carrying John Cleese, his back beautifully straight, gavottes down a London street. Viewers don’t know why he’s moving so ludicrously; it doesn’t matter. His silly walk undermines not only his dignified wardrobe but also the very idea of a professional persona. Just by changing his gait to something ridiculous, Cleese gives his audience a moment of release from the entire world of work. But sketch comedy, even when it has recurring characters, cannot develop those characters in the way a full-length work can. Its silliness, therefore, cannot have the emotional impact of the chicken-talk scene in Bocking, or the hot-dogs-as-fingers segment in the movie Everything Everywhere All at Once. One of that film’s many interwoven strands involves an alternate universe in which the characters played by Michelle Yeoh and Jamie Lee Curtis move through their days, experiencing life and love, with frankfurters for fingers. It’s a ridiculous conceit, pointless except to force a visceral sense of frustration, followed by acceptance, into their scenes together—which it does quite well, just as Tiana and Erickson’s bocking creates a visceral sense of relief. Silliness, in both cases, circumvents cerebral experience. No other means of getting a laugh goes so directly to the heart. Real silliness—serious silliness, if you will—lands in a sweet spot between the intellectualism of satire and the visual heft of spectacle. It strips away layers of adult logic, convention, and pretense, delivering grown-ups to the instinctive knowledge that toddlers possess: Work clothes are just costumes. Maneuvering a body through the world is both annoying and a cosmic joke. And communication, as Bocking so well demonstrates, often isn’t really about what gets said. Reasonably, this is tough for many writers, verbal creatures that we are, to admit, but in times of real emotional duress, words are rarely what counts. What matters are the feelings that lie underneath.
The Atlantic
The Artist Who Ripped Up the Rules of Drawing
2026-09-16

The Artist Who Ripped Up the Rules of Drawing

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Most retrospectives unfold like a stately procession. You buy your ticket and proceed through a choreographed sequence of galleries, scrutinizing first the juvenilia, then the promising early work. Next come the triumphal years, and finally (if the artist was lucky) a poignant late phase. An idea of timelessness prevails, enhanced by an enveloping atmosphere of climate control, security, and massive insurance coverage. The lighting is good; the frames are tasteful. Everything looks marvelous. There’s not a whiff of the urgency of the studio, no hint of the emotional or psychological state in which these objects were made. Lost is any suggestion of that feeling, deep inside the creator, of an improvised, inner necessity. “Willem de Kooning Drawing,” at the Art Institute of Chicago, is a classic retrospective—a decorous, deeply researched exhibition about a much-lauded artist at a major museum. In another, more robust sense, however, the show is a giant debauch. It’s frankly (and thrillingly) unseemly. I’ve never been to an art show that takes you so far inside the creative process. You start to wonder if you should even be permitted to get so close. “Art never seems to make me peaceful or pure,” De Kooning once said. “I always seem to be wrapped in the melodrama of vulgarity.” This show, more than any other De Kooning exhibit I’ve seen, delivers on the spirit of that oft-quoted statement. De Kooning, arguably the most widely revered of America’s 20th-century avant-garde artists, took the language of drawing, ripped it up, and rearranged it. Put that way, what he did sounds rebellious. It sounds punk. The exhibition, which will travel to the Rijksmuseum, in Amsterdam, in early October, embraces that spirit. But it has a more conservative purpose, which is to demonstrate that you can’t rip up art’s language effectively—or interestingly—without first really knowing it. De Kooning knew, from long study, how to draw contours. He knew how to create volume using gradations in tone or marks that move in certain ways, how to apply ink or paint with varying degrees of pressure and velocity, and how to employ color relations to suggest depth. Before you can throw all that technique up in the air and have it land again, you need to have the tools. De Kooning was both the best draftsman and the most lavishly gifted painter of the generation of postwar artists (among them Jackson Pollock, Lee Krasner, Mark Rothko, and Helen Frankenthaler) who helped move the art world’s center of gravity from Paris to New York. Born in the Netherlands in 1904, he trained, from the age of 13, as a commercial artist at the Rotterdam Academy of Visual Arts and Technical Sciences while apprenticing with a design firm. The show kicks off with a large drawing, Dish With Jugs, that De Kooning made at the academy. Working in charcoal and conté crayon two days a week for an entire year—600 hours—the young student achieved a remarkable level of photographic verisimilitude in the still life. As one of the exhibit’s curators, Kevin Salatino, writes of De Kooning’s trajectory in an introductory essay for the show’s catalog, the drawing became “the archetype of what he would forcibly reject while retaining the learned skills the work so brilliantly exemplifies, only now putting them to radically different purposes.” That De Kooning could draw like Ingres (or like Picasso during his Ingres-inspired period, from roughly 1917 to 1925) is amply demonstrated by other early works in the show, such as his adoring, fastidiously controlled pencil portrait of Elaine Fried, a fellow artist and his future wife. But, like a libertine desiring undiscovered pleasures, his appetite for new sensations only seemed to grow with time. Drawing has long been regarded as an intellectual activity, something generated, above all, by the mind, with its capacity for conceiving, imagining, and mandating. (You draw to design buildings and invent machines; you “draw up” battle plans and national borders.) “Drawing, as a way of thinking,” Salatino notes of De Kooning, “lay at the core of his practice, the root of everything.” But is it thought, as such, that we’re seeing in De Kooning’s drawings? The critic Peter Schjeldahl, who knew the artist, described him as “a hand with a man attached.” If you can attribute thought to a hand—and I’m willing to admit that you can, but it surely isn’t rational—then this show is indeed a record of thinking. But it may be better described as the record of some more mysterious melting of mind into body. De Kooning came to America as a stowaway in 1926. Residing first in Hoboken, New Jersey, for less than a year before settling in New York City, he remained without legal status for the next 35 years. During an extended period of hard graft as a house-and-sign painter, a window dresser, a show-card letterer, and an advertising-layout man, he encountered various avant-garde artists, including Arshile Gorky, who became a close friend. Under the Armenian American painter’s influence, he gradually shifted his focus from commercial art to modernist fine art, experimenting with different styles derived from Picasso, Miró, and Matisse. Even when ostensibly abstract, De Kooning’s work was populated by bobbling fragments of body parts. He conveyed these with his characteristic wobbling contours and flesh-textured paint. “Content is a glimpse of something, an encounter like a flash,” he once said. “It’s very tiny—very tiny, content.” He proceeded in the studio intuitively, precipitously, as if wanting to catch his subjects unawares. De Kooning had his first solo show in 1948. He broke through to widespread acclaim in 1950 with Excavation, a painting he labored over for many weeks, using a process of patient buildup and repeated erasure. At almost seven feet high and more than eight feet wide, Excavation was the largest canvas he’d ever worked on. (The following year, it was awarded a prize by the Art Institute of Chicago, which subsequently acquired it.) The work was initially inspired by scenes from an Italian movie, Bitter Rice, an art-house hit in Greenwich Village that year, showing young peasant women working in water-soaked fields. But over time, the bodies dissolved into their surroundings and the pictorial space flattened out into a scrim of fleetingly seen forms with hooked and curving contours that push forward from the surface or twist back into shallow space. If, in its genesis, the work recalled Picasso’s proto-cubist breakthrough, Les Demoiselles d’Avignon—the Spaniard’s 1907 painting of five naked women in a brothel—it also pointed the way to what would become a more explicit, and enduring, obsession for De Kooning: depictions of women, wobbly-edged, many of them leering or aggressive, disintegrating before our eyes into a welter of ambient marks. Inspired by prehistoric figurines of such powerful female divinities as the Venus of Willendorf, and by depictions of women by Rubens and Picasso—and riffing, too, on female figures in contemporary cartoons, movies, and magazine advertisements—De Kooning’s women take up a hefty portion of the show. The critic Harold Rosenberg, an early champion of his work, admired the classical balance of Excavation, likening it to “a formula wrung out of testing explosives.” Observing De Kooning drawing, another critic, Thomas Hess, noted that the artist “made a few strokes, then almost instinctively, it seemed to me, turned the pencil around and began to go over the graphite marks with the eraser. Not to rub out the lines but to move them, push them across the paper.” De Kooning’s line, he concluded, “is always under attack.” This ethos of creative destruction remained a guiding principle for De Kooning. The artist’s methods, however, continued to change. His drawings appear to be in a state of constant becoming. Shapes swell and fall away. They coalesce and unravel. De Kooning’s reversals of figure and ground aren’t binary or static, as in classic cubism; they’re in flux, like water moving in water. The show’s emphasis on the muck and mess of the studio may explain why artists in the Chicago area have flocked to the exhibit—and keep on returning, according to Mel Becker Solomon (who organized the show with Salatino, Margaret Holben Ellis, and Charlotte Healy). Solomon recounted to me that she walked through the exhibition with an artist, who told her, “This is what students are missing in their current practice: the tenacity and rigor to keep pushing, experimenting, and searching for what comes next.” What the show demonstrates, in ways that aren’t always obvious with his paintings, is that De Kooning never lost touch with the vocational skills he acquired as a young man. As Rosenberg wrote in 1962, “There was not a trick of the painter’s trade that de Kooning did not have up his sleeve.” Ellis, a paper conservator at the Art Institute of Chicago, lists those “tricks” in the catalog and explains how De Kooning adapted them to purposes for which they were never intended. She describes, for instance, his use of carbon-transfer paper and translucent vellum to copy motifs from one drawing to another. Such processes were typically used to duplicate technical drawings. But De Kooning would apply fragments of the reproductions to new compositions, often in different orientations. He also used frottage, or rubbing—another technique favored by commercial artists—to activate his lines. He would apply charcoal to thin vellum placed over corrugated cardboard or other textured surfaces to give the marks a stuttering look. “Frottage transformed stillness into speed,” Ellis writes of Reclining Nude, a 1963 drawing. This observation approaches the core of De Kooning’s art: More than any other artist I can think of, he gave painted marks and lines different velocities and trajectories. Co-existing in the same composition, those visible shifts in speed, thickness, and direction carved out three-dimensional spaces of ravishing complexity and urgency. Salatino suggests, in his catalog essay, that the artist’s use of frottage “functions, inescapably, as a metaphor for the masturbatory—making and meaning converging in an act dependent upon the probing, rubbing, exploring hand.” Salatino is a first-rate curator, but this sort of writing, to which his introduction is rather too prone, gives art writing a bad name. De Kooning didn’t traffic in metaphors. He wasn’t interested in meanings. There’s a reason Susan Sontag quoted him at the beginning of her essay “Against Interpretation.” Other techniques that De Kooning learned as a commercial artist included cutting out parts of images he found in magazines and inserting them into his compositions, or using masking tape and paper overlays to give his own marks abrupt, crisp boundaries. When drawing, De Kooning loved to use liner and sword brushes, which are favored by sign painters. Both have short handles and extended, bushy bristles. Loaded with ink and applied with control, they allow artists to make long, even lines. De Kooning had mastered their use as a younger man. (Ellis reports that the illustrator Gus Falk, who was taught by De Kooning at Black Mountain College, remembered him applying a perfectly even line to a spinning bicycle wheel by holding the brush steady. “He was in control,” Falk said. “He was interested in exactitude.”) But in his own drawing, De Kooning let rip with liner brushes, swishing them about all over the paper, achieving undulant lines that contract and expand like living creatures. Perhaps the most remarkable of De Kooning’s maladapted techniques was his use of fixatives. The word itself suggests De Kooning’s perversity. Fixatives are sprayed onto vulnerable media, such as charcoal and crayon, to stabilize drawn marks. But instead of spraying a fine, even mist from an appropriate distance, De Kooning aimed the fixative straight at his drawings from close range, creating puddles and rivulets that turned the paper gray, creating strange, smudgy marks and chemical textures. Later in his career, De Kooning tried drawing with his eyes closed or with both hands at once. Long plagued by alcoholism, he was often inebriated. “I’m in my element when I am a little bit out of this world,” he once said. “When I’m falling, I’m doing all right; when I’m slipping, I say, Hey, this is interesting!” The words could sound somewhat pathetic, like a drunk man’s sad, self-soothing rationalization. But the relationship between De Kooning’s virtuosity and the lengths to which he went just to get in his own way is intriguing to consider. Clement Greenberg, the most influential critic of the postwar period, framed the problem in a review of De Kooning’s first solo show. The artist’s “really great facility,” Greenberg argued, risked getting in the way of his ability to express authentic emotion with real originality. “Facility,” Greenberg argued, “has a stubbornness of its own and is loath to abandon easy satisfactions.” For Greenberg, “easy satisfactions” were always close to kitsch. But his assessment was reductive. De Kooning was an immigrant from Europe. His extensive training and virtuosic way with line connected him to an evolving tradition of European drawing that led from Tintoretto, Rubens, Delacroix, and Ingres to, in the modern era, Picasso and Matisse. He would never disown that tradition. But he was in America now. It was the 20th century. Young women were screaming in front of the Beatles. Crowds crammed into theaters to see Marilyn Monroe magnified on the screen. Betty Boop and Minnie Mouse bounced from frame to frame in the cartoons. De Kooning—neither calm nor pure and “wrapped,” as he had put it, “in the melodrama of vulgarity”—longed to incorporate all this impudent American energy into the authority of his European inheritance, and especially into his drawings and paintings of female figures. His efforts to interfere with his own facility were an expression both of his dividedness and of his deep hunger for freedom. Anyone who stands in front of De Kooning’s urgent, sensuously greedy drawings, with their writhing contours, cacophonous colors, and perverse processes, can feel the force of that craving.

['freedom', 'money without masters', 'world's hardest money', 'the future', 'here to stay', 'Hardest asset known to man', 'Pristine collateral', blah..blah]**" / "**Crypto Will End War**"   1. Whatever vague, un-qualifiable characteristic you apply to your magic spreadsheet numbers is cute, but just a bunch of marketing buzzwords with no real substance.   2. That which can be presented without evidence, can also be dismissed without evidence.   3. Talking in vague abstractions means you can make claims that nobody can actually test to see whether it's TRUE or FALSE. What does it even mean to say "money without masters?" (That's a rhetorical question.. our eyes would roll out of their sockets if you try to answer that.)   4. Calling something "The future" or "It's here to stay" seems to be more of a prayer or self-help-like affirmation than any statement of fact. The technical term is an *Unstated major premise fallacy*.   5. The argument that a crypto-based economy will "end war" makes zero sense. No war has ever ended because people ran out of money. Instead they ran out of *resources*. And during times of conflict, it's the use of power to acquire resources, not currency.   6. George Orwell did it better.#Stupid Crypto Talking Point #18 (Few Understand)"**You don't understand**" / "**DYOR**" / Using an insult in lieu of an argument.1. This is what we call, "[Crypto Gaslighting](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tspGVbmMmVA&t=571s)." Crypto proponents pretend that we're not smart enough to recognize the value of crypto, therefore there's something wrong with *us* and not the phony reality they're peddling.3. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
John Roberts’ New Role Model Demonstrates Everything Wrong With the Supreme Court
2026-09-16

John Roberts’ New Role Model Demonstrates Everything Wrong With the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. After the Supreme Court refused on Monday to let him disrupt mail-in voting, President Donald Trump denounced his own appointees and said the court’s recent rulings are so “shockingly bad” that “it won’t be easily possible for our Country to recover.” But if anything threatens the country’s recovery, it is the court’s other rulings, the ones that place the president above enacted law. The court has insulated the president from criminal laws designed to prevent corruption and election interference. It has let him disregard votes by Congress that prohibit him from starting illegal wars abroad. And it has said he can fire the watchdogs Congress installed to check him—replacing independent agency leaders with bootlickers. Yet because of decisions like the mail-ballot ruling, the court is often cast as the last line of defense between the presidency and autocracy. Such a view misunderstands what the court is up to in those rare instances when it has stood up to the president. In each of those cases, what we have witnessed is the court enforcing federal statutes. A judicial duty explicit in the Constitution, the enforcement of federal statutes is foundational to a nation where every individual, including the president, is subject to the rule of law. But alongside this important judicial role, the court has claimed a second, and more troubling function: a power to defy enacted federal law. Since the 1850s, the court has claimed that it may dissolve any statute Congress enacts whenever five justices dislike Congress’ constitutional judgment. The result has been an imperial presidency—along with a court that claims the exclusive power to police or condone the president’s excesses. To save our democracy from this unsustainable situation, the next Congress must replace judicial supremacy with a court accountable to federal law. A court required to enforce federal statutes would still strike down executive actions that violate the Constitution—indeed, a federal statute instructs judges to do precisely that. What the court would lose is a judicial power to immunize the president from the laws Congress has written to restrain him. After the Civil War, Congress began building a multiracial democracy in the United States. It passed civil rights acts that guaranteed birthright citizenship and enacted Reconstruction Acts that secured black suffrage in the South. Congress also faced a white-supremacist president, Andrew Johnson, determined to break its laws. Johnson commanded the military officials responsible for carrying out Reconstruction and he intended to sabotage it. So Congress bound him with legislation. The Command of the Army Act required presidential orders to flow through Gen. Ulysses S. Grant, whom Congress trusted, and it barred the president from removing Grant without the Senate’s approval. The Tenure of Office Act gave the same protection to the civilian officials in charge of Reconstruction. When Johnson appeared to violate that second law by firing Secretary of War Edwin Stanton, the House impeached him. During the impeachment trial in the Senate, Johnson’s lawyers made a novel and controversial claim. Even if Johnson had defied a federal statute, they argued, it was merely to allow the Supreme Court to decide its constitutionality. Members of Congress were horrified. They conceded that a president could object to the constitutionality of a bill by vetoing it during the lawmaking process—but they objected that the president could not wield a second veto by ignoring the law after it was enacted. A Constitution designed to free the people from monarchy would not have handed to the president this “more than kingly prerogative.” Johnson was spared by a single vote in the Senate. But he was also chastened. A president bent on defying federal law was restrained—not by nine justices, but by federal statutes and the representatives who enforced them. Reconstruction’s opponents drew a different lesson. They insisted that the president needed a court powerful enough to protect him from “the meshes” of Congress. As the nation abandoned Reconstruction, a generation of politicians and historians recast Johnson as a martyr to congressional tyranny. Their remedy was to articulate and entrench a new doctrine: that the Constitution makes the court supreme over Congress. They encouraged the court to void any statute that hems in presidential power. It took 50 years for that doctrine to triumph. It arrived carrying a grudge. In 1926, Chief Justice William Howard Taft wrote that studying “the injustice that the radical Republicans did to Andrew Johnson” left him “humiliated as a Republican.” He then used a case before him, Myers v. United States, to condemn the Tenure of Office Act and strike down a Reconstruction-era regulation of the presidency. Admirers cheered that the court had at last vindicated Andrew Johnson. Today’s court has resurrected that project. Just this term in Trump v. Slaughter, the court invoked Myers to shred century-old statutory protections that kept public servants from being replaced with loyalists. Afterward, on Substack, Chief Justice John Roberts wrote admiringly that Taft’s leadership not only protected the president from Congress, but also helped to fend off congressional efforts to rein in the court. Slaughter follows a dangerous pattern of opinions placing the president beyond the reach of enacted law. In Trump v. United States, the court exempted the president from most federal criminal law. The statutes that prohibit corruption, obstruction, and election interference now bind everyone in the country except its most powerful officer. With a subtle procedural order issued earlier this month, the court permitted the president to finish a $400 million ballroom on the rubble of the East Wing he demolished, a project two lower courts held he had no authority to build without Congress. Thanks to the court’s decisions kneecapping the Voting Rights Act and campaign-finance laws, the court has also unleashed dark money on our elections and prized partisan entrenchment over competitive elections and minority representation. Worse yet, the court has made clear it will not tolerate statutory responses to presidential overreach and corruption that any future majority might attempt. If we want a president who can be prosecuted for his crimes and who cannot purge the civil service or send masked agents into our neighborhoods, then Congress must be able to bind the presidency with laws that stick. Sticky laws require limiting the court’s power to dissolve them. Congress cannot use statutes to restrain a president whom the court exempts from duly enacted federal law. Some may object that the court has lately made itself the indispensable man. It struck down the president’s illegal tariffs. On Monday it refused to let the Postal Service impose new rules that would have kept millions of mail-in ballots from reaching voters. It appears poised to continue preserving birthright citizenship from the president’s attacks. Lower courts have blocked unlawful National Guard deployments and lawless Immigration and Customs Enforcement raids. Curb the judiciary now, the worry goes, and you disarm the last sentry on the wall. But look at which power the court is using in each case. Every one of these rulings restraining the president is enforcing a federal statute: the tariff decision enforced the law Congress wrote governing emergency powers; the mail-ballot order enforced the statute governing the Postal Service, which nowhere authorizes it to regulate elections; the birthright citizenship cases enforced a statutory guarantee Congress first enacted in 1866, even before the 14th Amendment; relief against ICE enforced the Administrative Procedure Act, a federal statute that instructs courts to vacate unconstitutional executive actions. By contrast, when the court has defied or rejected a federal statute because it doesn’t comport with how five justices see the Constitution, the court has loosened vital checks on presidential power—sometimes checks that many Congresses, and even many presidents, believed were both constitutional and crucial for a healthy democracy. These two types of judicial power are not two sides of one coin. They are in conflict. A court that defies federal law enables presidents to do the same. The choice we face today is not between a strong court and a weak one. It is between a court that puts the president above the law and a court that holds him to it. But to choose a president beholden to the rule of law, we need to hold the justices to enacted law as well. That choice is ours to make. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here

We Found the Most Galling Argument of the Upcoming Supreme Court Term
2026-09-16

We Found the Most Galling Argument of the Upcoming Supreme Court Term

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Today, more than 100 million Americans live in places where weapons like the AR-15 are banned or heavily restricted. Generations of ordinary citizens fought for these assault-weapon prohibitions in the wake of tragic killings on a mass scale. The gun-rights groups opposing these laws often had more intensity, more money, and more influence in legislative chambers. From the first statewide ban in 1989, motivated by a California school shooting that left five children dead, to Virginia’s passage of a ban earlier this year, the legislative victories were hard-won. Yet the laws in the 11 states that have these bans are suddenly on shaky ground. This fall, the Supreme Court will entertain a Second Amendment challenge to the prohibition of assault weapons in Viramontes v. Cook County. Briefing before the court is in full swing. The challengers—a few individuals who want to own such weapons, along with some gun-rights advocacy organizations—recently filed their brief with the justices, arguing that the Constitution prohibits the people of these states from protecting themselves through law. The challenge directly concerns bans in Connecticut and Cook County, Illinois, but the court’s ruling will apply nationwide. The legal side of the constitutional debate over assault weapons can become consumed with technical details, like muzzle velocity, rate of fire, and the amount of kinetic energy a bullet fired from these weapons transfers to a human body. Or with arcane historical trivia, like the magazine capacity of the air rifle Lewis and Clark took on their westward expedition in the early 1800s. There’s no doubt that measuring mechanical functionality and discerning historical fidelity have become critical components of the conversation. Yet, if left abstract, they can obscure the real human people by whom and for whom these laws were enacted. This abstraction turned to subterfuge in the challengers’ briefing before the Supreme Court last month. The most galling part of their argument is the recurrent invocation of—and self-righteous cloaking of their argument in—the language of democracy. “The decision whether a type of arm is too dangerous for ordinary Americans to possess,” they wrote, “is one that the people are entitled to make, not one that courts are empowered to make for them.” They say this, to be clear, in a brief asking the judges of the Supreme Court to make the decision about what weapons can be possessed over the decision of voters. Because the lower courts upheld these laws, the challengers want the justices to tell the citizens of Connecticut, Chicago, and other jurisdictions like California and New York that they cannot make fundamental judgments about public peace and safety. Later in the brief, the challengers again assert that “whether the danger posed by a particular type of firearm outweighs its utility for lawful purposes is a decision for the American people to make, and judges are not authorized to second-guess that decision.” Yet their precise argument depends upon judges second-guessing—indeed, overruling—the decision that millions of those American people made about these weapons. It seems that judges are empowered to second-guess the decision of the American people if, and only if, gun-rights proponents don’t like it. After all, the American people voted for these laws in more than a third of the country. Connecticut citizens saw an AR-15-style weapon slaughter innocent 6-year-olds and acted to expand their law. The challengers, on the other hand, would have the Supreme Court foreclose that pathway of democratic choice. Contemporary Americans have grown accustomed to viewing the Supreme Court as the ultimate check on majority rule. And, in some areas, it might appropriately act that way. But—to the extent one credits the founders’ views—there’s good reason to doubt they thought nine lawyers in robes should have the unbounded authority to overrule a democratically elected legislature’s choice about how best to balance public good and individual liberty. It distorts the concept of democracy to cloak oneself in the rhetoric of protecting decisions by “the people” while urging an insulated cadre of educated attorneys to tell the parents who buried their children and then devoted their lives to the prevention of gun violence to shut up and sit down; your voice and your vote do not matter here, the challengers demand the court to decree. It would be a tragedy for the court to follow that path in the Viramontes case. It is an approach that is both historically uninformed and normatively unattractive. It squelches the most important right the founders fought to preserve to their posterity: the right to rule ourselves. Fortunately, federal judges have by and large rejected these calls to restrict the people’s right to make gun laws. No challenge to an assault-weapon ban prevailed among the federal courts of appeals until after the Supreme Court agreed to hear Viramontes. Conservative federal Judge J. Harvie Wilkinson expressed the matter best when he wrote for a majority of judges upholding Maryland’s assault-weapon ban: “We shudder to imagine the hubris with which a court would disable representative government at the very moment that lethal technologies are proceeding at an accelerated and indeed unprecedented pace.” As we await its decision, one can only hope there are enough Supreme Court justices still willing to don the humility required to let the people remain their own rulers. Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms
2026-09-15

Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. On Monday, the Supreme Court blocked Donald Trump’s effort to throw mail balloting into chaos ahead of the November midterm elections. But that was just one prong of Trump’s attempts to tilt races toward Republicans. Today we’ll explain the others. This Supreme Court ruling seems like a big deal. What exactly happened? You aren’t wrong. As the legal expert Richard Hasen explains in Slate, Trump’s executive order would have created onerous new requirements for mail-in voting that risked disenfranchising millions of elderly, military, and other voters. Seven of nine justices apparently saw that potential catastrophe coming and rejected it, prompting a Tuesday-morning tantrum from the president. Trump has repeatedly tried to get the court to take his side on mail-in ballots, but this ruling looks like the end of the line—at least for this year’s elections. OK, bullet dodged. But what else is Trump doing that we should worry about? Trump’s order was backed by his unfounded claims that mail voting is fraudulent, part of his broader obsession with the false notion that Democrats are “cheating” to win elections. Trump has said he wants to “take over” elections, which are currently administered by state-level officials. That decentralized system is actually a bulwark against the kind of cheating Trump claims to care about—and against a sore-loser president trying to change election rules to protect his own interests. Still, Trump’s administration is trying to get states to hand over voter data and hunting through the rolls for alleged fraud. It’s true that individuals have sometimes illegally cast ballots. But many of those cases have been accidents, like when a software error in New Jersey improperly registered noncitizens in 2023 and 2024, and widespread fraud is a myth. Per the Heritage Foundation, a conservative think tank that helped write Project 2025, there have been about 100 cases of noncitizen voting over the past 25 years. Nevertheless, the administration has asked almost every state to turn over databases that include voters’ personal information. Most have resisted, sparking court battles that the administration has overwhelmingly lost. The Department of Homeland Security, meanwhile, has assigned hundreds of employees to parse public voting databases for noncitizens. Some have allegedly posed as voters, potentially violating state privacy laws. Yet Secretary Markwayne Mullin has publicly touted unverified numbers of alleged noncitizen voters and threatened election officials with prison time. But surely those efforts can be contested in court? Democrats and voting-rights groups have already sued over some of Trump’s moves (hence the case rejecting his mail ballot order). But some fear the administration is looking for ways to circumvent courts. Late last month, a whistleblower alleged that the U.S. Postal Service had continued to build an online portal that could’ve thrown out mail ballots en masse despite an order blocking work on it. (The Postal Service claims it hasn’t defied any court orders.) Courts can’t stop Trump from sowing doubt about the soundness of American elections. He has ordered investigations into debunked voting irregularities in states he lost in 2020 and continues to attack voting machines despite there being no evidence that they’ve ever changed election results. That could lay the groundwork to claim there was fraud if certain races don’t go Republicans’ way this fall. And even if judges toss those claims, they may still depress faith in the results. One recent survey found that less than half of Americans expect the midterms to be free and fair, with most Republicans doubting that mailed ballots are counted accurately and most Democrats fearful that Trump will deploy immigration agents to polling places. So, while the voting could ultimately go off without a hitch, the crisis of confidence continues. The midterms are still several weeks away, so let’s turn to a more urgent question: How to enjoy the rest of your evening. My colleagues have some ideas that don’t require an appeal to the Supreme Court. Take a sip: Martinis are savored for their elegant simplicity and refined romanticism. Now their iconic imagery has been repurposed as chintzy merch and wine mom–style decor. Courtney Iseman asks, “When did we as a society collectively decide, We martini, therefore we are?” Reconsider your “smart” home: Cameras are becoming more and more common in home appliances, including in smart ovens, refrigerators, doorbells, and toothbrushes—even as people decry surveillance measures in public. What does this trend mean for our consumption? Steffi Cao has a prediction. Give your brain a workout: What’s an 11-letter “Enya hit with the repeated lyric ‘Sail away, sail away, sail away’ ”? What’s a nine-letter “cutesy term for spooky snakes”? Sharpen your wordplay skills with today’s crossword. Celebrate with Slate: In honor of our 30th birthday, Slate has revived our original 1996 homepage and filled it with some of our favorite stories. Revisit articles by Slate alums Daniel Engber, David Plotz, Emily Yoffe, and more. We’ll be updating this page over the next month, so keep an eye out for more pieces from the vault! Slate was one of the first news outlets I read regularly, so I’m really enjoying the birthday throwback. Happy trails back to 1996, and hope you have a great night! News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein
2026-09-15

Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The specter of Jeffrey Epstein continues to haunt the Trump administration seven years after the sexual predator’s death. But the makings of the next Epstein-style scandal—one that ultimately could be even more damaging for Trump and MAGAworld—appears to be unfolding right now. The Tate brothers, Andrew and Tristan, are alleged to be sex predators on a level approaching Epstein and with perhaps even more political clout with Donald Trump. For those who are blessedly unfamiliar with the Tates, they are highly popular “manosphere” influencers who have garnered an international following, particularly among young men, for their particular brand of content focused on their prideful misogyny and flashing of their apparent wealth. They are also accused of running a decadelong international sex-trafficking ring, with credible allegations against them in multiple countries from dozens of accusers involving gruesome acts of sexual violence, abuse, and exploitation against women and minors. And they have many connections to the world’s top political elite, including Donald Trump’s family members and close advisers. Sound familiar? Just like Epstein, the Tates have seemingly leveraged these connections to receive favorable treatment and avoid the full extent of justice. After years of investigations and attempted prosecutions, the Tates find themselves in the crosshairs yet again. In July, they were charged in Britain on rape charges that also included allegations of possession of child sexual abuse imagery—and are currently sitting in a jail in Miami, fighting extradition. And earlier this month, Romanian prosecutors indicted Andrew for trafficking minors, money laundering, sex with a minor, and witness tampering, and Tristan with complicity. But, in a few important ways, the involvement of Trump’s administration and allies with the Tates could be even more egregious than the Epstein controversy. While the Epstein scandal has centered on holding bad actors accountable for past actions and current cover-ups, the Tate brothers’ scandal is a live issue, and there is reason to believe that if the Trump administration buckles in their favor, the brothers could go back to their criminal activities. Unlike Epstein, who generally lurked in the shadows of elite circles, the Tates brazenly and publicly broadcast their depraved behavior for years. The Tates established their popularity by building online and porn businesses, and becoming internet sensations through their aggressive and controversial misogynistic opinions. Indicia of their alleged criminality have been publicly available for years, including Andrew attacking a woman on camera in 2016. In the process, they lured in young women and allegedly sexually abused and raped them. The victims allegedly include minors. Accusations and law enforcement investigations of rape and strangulation have been swirling around the Tates since at least 2015, when they relocated from the U.K. to Romania to escape possible arrest. Rape allegations persisted in the subsequent years, as the Tates continued to grow their online following. In 2023, the Tates and several co-conspirators were formally charged by Romanian authorities, with allegations from about 35 alleged victims, including minors. The Tate brothers were barred from leaving Romania while the prosecution continued, having been labeled flight risks and public dangers. (The Tate brothers deny all allegations of criminal conduct.) Despite this, their following grew. And, just as Epstein used his wealth and social status to develop political relationships, the Tate brothers’ rise in popularity has come with an increase in political influence, which they have eagerly flaunted. The connections between the Tates and Trumpworld multiplied over time, even as accusations against them piled up. They developed a relationship with the president’s youngest son, Barron, who encouraged his father to engage with the manosphere podcasts and ecosystem. They were personally in touch with Donald Trump Jr., who livestreamed with Andrew in 2024 and called the Tates’ Romanian detention in 2023 “absolute insanity.” (The White House has not responded to questions about Barron’s relationship with the Tates.) After the 2024 election, shows of support from top Trump advisers became even louder. Vice President–elect J.D. Vance followed the Tates on X; right before Trump’s inauguration, incoming counselor to the president Alina Habba said she was a “big fan” of the Tates, compared their legal issues to Trump’s, and told Andrew, “If you’re in the States, I got your back.” After Trump took office, one of the Tates’ former lawyers, Paul Ingrassia, became the White House’s liaison to the Justice Department and then the Department of Homeland Security. These connections appeared to pay off almost immediately after Trump’s inauguration. In February 2025, Richard Grenell, the Trump administration’s special envoy, attended the Munich Security Conference with Vice President Vance and seemed to push for the Tates’ release. Vance’s speech to the conference, in part, echoed right-wing critiques of a recent Romanian judicial decision. Meanwhile, Grenell, after being lobbied by the Tates’ PR team to help the brothers, held a side meeting with Romanian officials at the conference in which he reportedly asked how “the Tate boys” were doing. The New Yorker reported that it seemed clear to the Romanian foreign minister that the Americans were supporting the Tates because the Tates backed Trump in 2024, and that the Tates were “playing a role within the bilateral relations” between the two countries. (The White House denied this, and Grenell denied helping the Tates, but has publicly stated: “I support the Tate brothers.”) Nevertheless, Grenell’s and Vance’s behavior appears to have been interpreted as subtle but clear pressure from the Trump administration to release the Tates. Which is precisely what happened days later. Romania lifted the travel restrictions, in the face of fierce opposition by prosecutors, and the brothers gallivanted to meet their MAGA friends in the United States. When they arrived in Miami, Trump’s oldest living political svengali Roger Stone posted a picture with them and wrote: “Richard Grenell secured the release of the Tates.” According to the New Yorker, Andrew “denied that Donald Trump had personally intervened, but said he had learned from powerful people in Bucharest that the restrictions were removed because of American pressure.” With the Tates now finding themselves in law enforcement’s dragnet yet again, the parallels with Epstein are striking. Although the Department of Justice arrested the Tates in July 2026, assisting the U.K.’s extradition efforts under the new criminal charges, and the White House said they wouldn’t get involved in the matter, the brothers are continuing to try to use their influence to pressure the Trump administration. Following the arrest, the Tates’ lawyer said: “Andrew Tate was a material participant in ensuring President Trump’s victory. And if Trump doesn’t support him, and if the next people who are going to be running for office potentially are just like, yeah, down the river he goes, that young cohort of people will break with the Republican Party in the next election.” As the Epstein scandal has taught us, the fact that Trump’s DOJ arrested the Tates does not lessen the severity of what has already transpired, or the risks going forward. The last time the Tates wriggled away from custody and came to the U.S., a woman in California accused Andrew of choking her and battering her head and face; after their encounter, she was diagnosed with post-concussion syndrome. L.A. prosecutors did not ultimately bring charges but she is pursuing a lawsuit against him, including as evidence her medical report and texts in which he threatened to beat her. The same dynamic transpired with Epstein two decades ago: He, too, was arrested and charged but continued to put significant pressure on the government and ultimately received a sweetheart deal. He then went back to his life of luxury and sex crimes. We’ve already seen the Trump administration take aggressive steps to help supporters who have been charged with crimes and convicted, so his administration’s possible involvement in the Tate brothers’ story isn’t necessarily over. The Tates are continuing to challenge their extradition, and ultimately, once they exhaust their legal options, whether they are extradited to face their charges or not would ultimately be up to the Trump administration. The scope of Epstein’s criminal behavior, and the full extent of his social network, was largely ignored while he was at large. Today, the Tates and the apparent depths of their interlocking connections to people around Trump have broadly fallen under the radar of mainstream media coverage. But for younger Americans they are totemic figures in this political and cultural moment, and their influence is particularly pernicious. That’s why there must be full transparency and congressional investigations to unravel what has already happened and expose the full extent of their relationship with Trumpworld, and also to uncover what, if any, efforts are continuing to be made now by their allies to pressure the Trump administration as the Tates continue to face charges in the United Kingdom and Romania. As a member of the legal team representing the victims of Jeffrey Epstein in litigation, I can attest that the struggle to achieve justice after his death has been righteous but hard. The Tates must be held accountable for their actions and neither favor nor fame can be allowed to shield them. The Epstein scandal and what happened to his victims is heinous; the Trump administration had begun to repeat that miscarriage of justice. Hopefully, they won’t be allowed to continue. Enterprises are sweating legacy IT assets as AI investment grows
2026-09-16

Enterprises are sweating legacy IT assets as AI investment grows

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Hardware such as mainframes found to hold the data and business logic needed to build those AI services Companies are getting more selective about replacing legacy kit such as mainframe systems, and rising AI investment is one of the factors causing them to hold onto these existing assets for longer. Managed services biz Ensono claims in its 2026 State of IT Modernization report that 78 percent of IT decision makers regard legacy systems as more important today than they did two years ago, and this is because such systems are viewed as key to making AI work for their organization. The report is based on a survey of 500 IT decision-makers and line-of-business leaders conducted by the firm across the US and UK. Its findings show that AI is reshaping modernization priorities, but that corporates are still grappling with many of the challenges that have traditionally complicated modernization efforts, such as budget overruns, delayed initiatives, and talent shortages. Ensono found that 45 percent of firms are actively scaling AI deployments across their organization, while 44 percent are investing in targeted, high-impact use cases. Supporting AI, automation, and advanced data initiatives is now the top pressure driving modernization, while more than half of companies say that AI is helping advance modernization by delivering through greater automation and improved efficiency. Among the barriers businesses face, the top challenges to achieving AI goals are said to be difficulty integrating AI into existing workflows and business processes (33 percent of respondents) and infrastructure limitations (28 percent). For this reason, Ensono says that those legacy systems are taking on new strategic value as organizations attempt to scale their AI rollout. Nearly half of organizations view legacy systems such as mainframes as a critical foundation for AI and an important source of data for this purpose, while 47 percent use them selectively for specific AI applications. More than half of organizations are optimizing and extending legacy systems while modernizing applications where they are, rather than replacing them outright. This is an approach that’s slightly more common in the UK (57 percent of respondents) than the US (48 percent). “Enterprises are discovering that legacy systems, like the mainframe, are intensely powerful, reliable and efficient sources of computing that can now be augmented and made more agile thanks to AI,” says Ensono’s Chief Strategy Officer Brian Klingbeil. “These systems contain decades of data and business logic that drive many companies. The advantage will go to organizations that understand their environments well enough to distinguish what should be replaced versus what should be augmented, and embrace new modernization methods that simply were not available even 18 months ago.” However, using the mainframe as a foundation for AI rollouts was noted by infrastructure services firm Kyndryl two years ago. It found that big iron was becoming a prime candidate to host and run AI workloads, while enterprises were increasingly integrating their mainframes with modern infrastructure. This meant moving some workloads off the mainframe while updating others in place to continue to benefit from the security and reliability of the platform. Analyst Gartner also said earlier this year that migrating workloads to a mainframe made more sense for VMware users than adopting Broadcom’s new licenses. Elsewhere, HPE managing director for UK, Middle East and Africa said that enterprise customers are opting to sweat IT assets for longer, moving refresh cycles from five years to seven years, for example. “I think we're seeing earlier planning for critical projects. I think a lot of customers are looking much further ahead on their roadmap of spend at the things they have to do, because maybe some technology is going end of support or the lease is expiring at the datacenter,” he said. “So I think you're seeing a decision tree of, do we pause here, and do we sweat the asset for a little bit longer, or is this essential to move ahead? I think it's fundamentally forcing a rethink for customers.” ® Hardware such as mainframes found to hold the data and business logic needed to build those AI services He'll need to earn around $13K a year to pay pops back between now and when he turns 18, so at least there's that free labor to look forward to PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Kmscon brings smooth scaling, richer colors, and better Unicode support beneath GNOME 51 Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Nine-year-old runs up $118,000 bill on dad's corporate credit card advertising his Roblox YouTube channel
2026-09-16

Nine-year-old runs up $118,000 bill on dad's corporate credit card advertising his Roblox YouTube channel

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
He'll need to earn around $13K a year to pay pops back between now and when he turns 18, so at least there's that free labor to look forward to There’s kids running up a few hundred bucks on in-app purchases, and then there’s the case of nine-year old YouTuber Mighty Mike Plays, who just cost his dad $118,000 – and possibly his job – after running a bunch of Google ad campaigns for his channel on papa's company credit card. The young Roblox and Minecraft YouTuber posted a video Tuesday – or perhaps it's more apt to say his father Dave posted the video to explain why Might Mike Plays might be going dark for a while. In it, nine-year-old Mike explains “I think this might be the end,” before handing the mic to his dad. “My son has been running some high-budget campaigns on YouTube that I was pretty much unaware of,” Dave explained after taking the mic. Dave didn’t figure this out after spotting the charges himself, however. He learned about it after being called into a meeting with his manager and the finance department at his employer, an unnamed “large” company where he works in marketing. Dave was confronted with a spreadsheet with a full breakdown of three weeks of advertising spending from his corporate card that totaled $118K and asked to explain it. “It dawned on me that, oh crap, this is probably something my son did,” Dave said in the video. “The number sitting on the page in front of me had about five more digits than it should have.” A breakdown of the spending named his child’s account, as well as Roblox and Minecraft video titles, making it pretty clear pretty quickly that Mighty Mike had been misusing dad’s corporate card. Dave breaks down Mighty Mike's spending spree Whatever excuse Dave made for the mortgage-sized mess apparently didn’t go over too well with his employer, as Dave explained in the video that the matter is being escalated up the corporate ladder and he’s not sure if he’ll keep his job. “This is not just like a haha, funny story, everything worked out fine kinda situation,” Dave said. “There is a real genuine chance I have to end up personally having to pay some or all of this back out of my pocket.” As for how a nine-year old could run up six figures of ad spending to boost his video game recordings, Dave admits that’s pretty much entirely his fault. Dave started the channel for Mike a couple of years ago after watching his son spend hours in front of the computer passively ingesting Roblox and Minecraft videos posted by other players. He reasoned that encouraging Mike to record and post his own videos would at least be a bit more interactive. After a few months of doing so, Mike asked his dad for some Robux, the in-game currency used by Roblox players to buy cosmetic items and access to game features. In what would prove to be the first of several dumb moves, Dave put it on his company card. The advertising professional apparently failed to realize that putting those card details into the account made them permanently accessible. Then came the second mistake: Mike said that his subscriber and viewer count wasn’t growing as fast as he wanted it to, so Dave sat him down and helped him set up a Google ad campaign for his videos with a $20 spending limit. “I didn’t think for one second that this was going to be an issue,” Dave admitted. “Huge mistake on my part; turns out he was watching a little more closely than I realized.” After that $20 budget ran out, Mike simply started another campaign. Then another, and another, and presumably a bunch more after that, too. “When a nine-year old gets access to ad account with no limits and a CC that just says ‘approved’ every single time no matter what, he does not think that what he’s doing is wrong,” Dave said, correctly taking the blame for everything that happened. “This nine-year-old kid literally ransacked my company card,” Dave added. There are some good lessons for parents to take away from this: First off, don’t use your company card for personal purchases, which ought to go without saying. It’s perfectly reasonable that this should cost Dave his job, as he was apparently fine with using his company card for small personal purchases and is only feeling the sting of accountability because his kid went wild with company cash. Second, it’s probably safe to assume, after so many in-app purchase horror stories over the years, that any financial information stored on a device accessible to a kid is going to be used inappropriately, and you have no one to blame except yourself when it happens. “I’m not blaming him,” said Dave. “He’s only nine, he didn’t understand fully what a company card meant, or that a large number on the screen represented real money.” Better yet, just keep your kids away from screens. There’s plenty of evidence to suggest exposing kids to screen time – even a bit of it – has a long-term effect on their developing brains. Make ‘em go play outside, and if Minecraft is still on their minds just give ‘em a shovel and tell them to dig until they find some diamonds, or redstone, or something. It’s better for your wallet, the kids get exercise, and on the off chance they find something of value you can turn that into some Robux instead. ® Hardware such as mainframes found to hold the data and business logic needed to build those AI services He'll need to earn around $13K a year to pay pops back between now and when he turns 18, so at least there's that free labor to look forward to PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Kmscon brings smooth scaling, richer colors, and better Unicode support beneath GNOME 51 Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Fedora 45 beta drags the Linux console into the 21st century
2026-09-16

Fedora 45 beta drags the Linux console into the 21st century

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Kmscon brings smooth scaling, richer colors, and better Unicode support beneath GNOME 51 Fedora Linux 45 beta has arrived with Linux kernel 7.2, a release-candidate build of GNOME 51, and extensive changes beneath the desktop. The biggest surprise is that Linux's legacy in-kernel console – the text-mode interface normally hidden beneath the GUI – has been replaced with a software-controlled alternative. The replacement is kmscon, a userspace terminal emulator that has been in development for more than a decade. Its Freedesktop project page explains that it uses the kernel's Direct Rendering Manager (DRM) and Kernel Mode Setting (KMS) facilities to configure the display, while offering better internationalization support. Kmscon was announced way back in 2012, but after a few years' work, developer David Herrmann moved on to other things. Fortunately, another developer, Peifeng Yu – nicknamed Aetf – maintained a fork for a decade until the original team resumed work last year. Some of the benefits of kmscon may sound a little theoretical: it has better Unicode support, which will help people working with scripts and alphabets beyond plain old US English, and it has multiseat support, allowing multiple users to share one PC simultaneously with separate keyboards and displays. We suspect that this isn't so common in 2026, but it could be useful to someone somewhere. We first heard of the advantages of kmscon via a blog post about Veronica Explains' writer deck: she uses it to display more than 16 colors on the console, and it also adds the ability to zoom in and out on the fly in Linux text mode. That does sound useful to us, especially with modern HiDPI screens. GNOME 51 is not yet final, so the closest thing to release notes is a fairly uninformative change log. It doesn't look to be a very exciting release, but there are improvements to keyboard navigation and the Orca screen reader, automatic screen rotation on devices with orientation sensors, refinements in the Settings app, Files, Calculator, and the login screen, and more. KDE users get Plasma 6.7. The browser-based installer now offers initial installation flows for the immutable Atomic Desktops and supports partitioning with Red Hat's Stratis storage system, which we looked at back in 2022. Under the hood, the RPM package manager now enforces package signing, and the higher-level DNF5 packaging tool now avoids switching package vendors during upgrades – which should prevent one category of unpleasant surprise. Atomic Desktop ISO builds now use image-builder, while Fedora CoreOS gains zram memory compression and the systemd-oomd memory pressure handler, although, as we reported last year, the latter is not universally popular. Fedora 45's security tooling has been updated and tweaked in several areas, and as usual, all the various programming languages and runtimes have been updated. Fedora Asahi Remix 45 is also in beta and, as The Register covered earlier this month, it includes preliminary support for Apple's M3, M3 Pro, and M3 Max chips, although performant 3D GPU acceleration is not yet available. That is good news for anyone running a headless system, but less useful to desktop and laptop users. For now, though, there is no Asahi Linux support for Apple's M4 and M5 systems. We briefly tried the Fedora Workstation beta under VMware, and it doesn't look very different from before – but we very much like the ability to resize console text with Ctrl and + or -. Even at larger sizes, text remains smooth and sharp. It may be a small change, but we suspect plenty of users will appreciate it. ® Hardware such as mainframes found to hold the data and business logic needed to build those AI services He'll need to earn around $13K a year to pay pops back between now and when he turns 18, so at least there's that free labor to look forward to PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Kmscon brings smooth scaling, richer colors, and better Unicode support beneath GNOME 51 Downloading a model is increasingly easy. SpaceX aims Starship for orbit on September 22
2026-09-16

SpaceX aims Starship for orbit on September 22

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Flight test 14 angling for 10 hours in space and a splashdown in the Pacific SpaceX is aiming for a September 22 launch of its monstrous Starship rocket on a 10-hour mission to deliver 26 Starlink V3 satellites into orbit. The flight, set to launch at 1215 UTC if all goes to plan and the authorities give the green light, will be the first time Starship has gone into orbit, and the mission is planned to fly at an altitude of approximately 275 km. The mission aims for six orbits, followed by a deorbit burn using a single Raptor engine while. The engineers are then planning a splashdown in the Pacific Ocean west of Chile. The Super Heavy Booster will be ditched in the Gulf of Mexico, hopefully under better control this time. On flight 13, the booster made a "hard splashdown," as SpaceX delicately put it, after only eight of the planned 13 engines reignited. The booster used all 33 engines for its boostback burn, but according to SpaceX, "the three center engines showed signs of ice clogging which triggered an early end to the maneuver." In true iterative style, SpaceX tweaked the hardware to improve filtering and updated the software to "enhance relight reliability." SpaceX has also changed Starship's heatshield, including adding curved tiles to reduce heating in tile gaps and "additional retention mechanisms added to tiles in areas deemed to be at highest risk of falling off during ascent." It is also reusing a pair of tiles recovered from the previous flight test. If Starship is to become truly reusable, these tiles will need to fly over and over again. Getting to orbit is by no means guaranteed. The SpaceX team will only command a final burn to enter orbit if there is sufficient hardware redundancy to perform a deorbit burn when the time comes. After all, nobody wants something the size of Starship making an uncontrolled reentry after its orbit eventually decays. Starship has had an eventful test program. Some missions were spectacularly successful, and several boosters returned to be caught by arms on the launch tower. Others were impressive failures. The vehicle is critical to SpaceX's future; it can loft far greater payloads than the company's workhorse Falcon 9 and is an essential part of NASA's Artemis program. For Artemis III, planned for 2027, SpaceX is expected to launch a Starship equipped with the docking apparatus planned for the lunar variant. Reaching orbit is therefore a critical step. ® Hardware such as mainframes found to hold the data and business logic needed to build those AI services He'll need to earn around $13K a year to pay pops back between now and when he turns 18, so at least there's that free labor to look forward to PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Kmscon brings smooth scaling, richer colors, and better Unicode support beneath GNOME 51 Downloading a model is increasingly easy.
